I mean… “afraid” sounds dramatic, but yeah, kind of. Then I’m already thinking about what I need to cut back the next day.\n\nWhich is stupid, because half the time those dinners are basically part of the job—everyone ordering appetizers, drinks, the whole show—and you can’t be the weird guy making a scene about it. But then afterward it’s all I can think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat due to this fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Most days I keep it pretty tight—coffee in the morning, then maybe a protein bar if I’m between sales calls and need something quick. Honestly, half the time I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy, which I am, like when I’m driving all over to meet accounts and you don’t want to show up smelling like takeout or whatever.\n\nBut then at night I’ll usually do something small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. Because once I sit down and eat a full meal, it feels like I’ve blown the whole day, like I lost the one thing I was keeping under control.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of gaining weight affect other areas of your life beyond just what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I still do my route, I’m not falling apart at work or anything. I’m still seeing accounts, making calls, doing the whole sales thing.\n\nBut it does kind of bleed into stuff. Like client dinners are a pain now—unless I know the place or can sort of steer where we go, I’ll make an excuse. Same with dates, honestly. Which is weird because after the divorce everyone’s like, “Get back out there,” and my buddy keeps trying to set me up with this woman he knows, and I keep saying I’m slammed with work.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more… mostly when I can’t control what the food situation’s going to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean… normal is kind of a weird word. Like, I know everyone’s making a big deal about it lately, and yeah, maybe I’m on the thinner side for my height.

But I don’t look at it like some medical emergency. I’m still jogging, I’m still out working my territory, seeing accounts, hitting my numbers. If I were really that far off, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to keep up with all that, you know?

I had this one client last week—totally unrelated—but he was telling me how he’s trying to “get healthy” and cut carbs, and everyone acts like that’s discipline when it’s him. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… maybe. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, obviously, but I do think I’m probably looking at different things than they are.

Like if I’m getting ready for a meeting, or especially a date, I’ll catch myself in the mirror and I’m not thinking, “Oh, you look fine.” I’m looking right at my stomach, or my face, like whether my face looks puffy or whether my shirt makes me look… I don’t know, soft. And if I get that in my head, it’s hard to shake. I start thinking people are going to read that as sloppy, or like I don’t have control over myself.

Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because most people are probably thinking about their own stuff. Like half my clients barely look up from their phones unless we’re talking pricing. But in the moment it feels like that’s what they’re going to notice.

But yeah… my weight’s kept dropping since the divorce, and everyone’s acting like it’s some big alarm bell.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds dramatic, but yeah, kind of. If I get on the scale after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami and it’s even a pound up, I get this rush of panic, like I’ve messed everything up or I’m losing control. Then I’m already thinking about what I need to cut back the next day.\n\nWhich is stupid, because half the time those dinners are basically part of the job—everyone ordering appetizers, drinks, the whole show—and you can’t be the weird guy making a scene about it. But then afterward it’s all I can think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat due to this fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Most days I keep it pretty tight—coffee in the morning, then maybe a protein bar if I’m between sales calls and need something quick. Honestly, half the time I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy, which I am, like when I’m driving all over to meet accounts and you don’t want to show up smelling like takeout or whatever.\n\nBut then at night I’ll usually do something small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. Because once I sit down and eat a full meal, it feels like I’ve blown the whole day, like I lost the one thing I was keeping under control.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of gaining weight affect other areas of your life beyond just what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I still do my route, I’m not falling apart at work or anything. I’m still seeing accounts, making calls, doing the whole sales thing.\n\nBut it does kind of bleed into stuff. Like client dinners are a pain now—unless I know the place or can sort of steer where we go, I’ll make an excuse. Same with dates, honestly. Which is weird because after the divorce everyone’s like, “Get back out there,” and my buddy keeps trying to set me up with this woman he knows, and I keep saying I’m slammed with work.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more… mostly when I can’t control what the food situation’s going to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… people keep saying I look too thin, so I know that’s what they think. My sister made this whole thing out of it last time I saw her, and even one of my accounts joked that I was “shrinking,” which was awkward.\n\nBut underweight, like medically? I don’t know. I still get up, I still jog, I’m still driving my territory and hitting my numbers. If I can do all that, it doesn’t really feel like some emergency, you know? Like, I’m functioning. So I guess I don’t totally see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal is kind of a weird word. Like, I know everyone’s making a big deal about it lately, and yeah, maybe I’m on the thinner side for my height.\n\nBut I don’t look at it like some medical emergency. I’m still jogging, I’m still out working my territory, seeing accounts, hitting my numbers. If I were really that far off, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to keep up with all that, you know?\n\nI had this one client last week—totally unrelated—but he was telling me how he’s trying to “get healthy” and cut carbs, and everyone acts like that’s discipline when it’s him. But with me, suddenly it’s a problem. So… I don’t know. Maybe not “normal” normal, but I don’t think it’s as bad as everyone keeps saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, obviously, but I do think I’m probably looking at different things than they are.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting, or especially a date, I’ll catch myself in the mirror and I’m not thinking, “Oh, you look fine.” I’m looking right at my stomach, or my face, like whether my face looks puffy or whether my shirt makes me look… I don’t know, soft. And if I get that in my head, it’s hard to shake. I start thinking people are going to read that as sloppy, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nWhich sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because most people are probably thinking about their own stuff. Like half my clients barely look up from their phones unless we’re talking pricing. But in the moment it feels like that’s what they’re going to notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah… I think I probably would. I mean, the mirror and the scale make it worse, definitely, because then there’s like proof sitting there staring at me.

But even if I didn’t have them, I’d still be thinking about it. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve been out for a weekend in Miami or something, I can just feel this panic start up, like, “Okay, you overdid it, you’re losing control.” And if the scale is even one pound higher after that, it kind of confirms it, and then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.

It’s annoying because those dinners are part of the job sometimes. You can’t sit there with a buyer at a steakhouse and make it weird, you know? They’re ordering appetizers, drinks, dessert, and you’re trying to keep the relationship smooth. But then later I’m not thinking about the account, I’m thinking about how to undo it. So… yeah, I don’t think taking away the mirror would totally take away the worry.

I start thinking people are going to read that as sloppy, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nWhich sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because most people are probably thinking about their own stuff. Like half my clients barely look up from their phones unless we’re talking pricing. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve been out for a weekend in Miami or something, I can just feel this panic start up, like, “Okay, you overdid it, you’re losing control.” And if the scale is even one pound higher after that, it kind of confirms it, and then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nIt’s annoying because those dinners are part of the job sometimes. You can’t sit there with a buyer at a steakhouse and make it weird, you know? They’re ordering appetizers, drinks, dessert, and you’re trying to keep the relationship smooth. I don’t know if there’s like… a number where I’d say, “That’s perfect.” It’s more a look, I guess.

Like before a meeting or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not looking at the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look kind of soft or puffy. And if I think I do, it gets in my head that people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have control.

Which is probably unfair—like, I had this buyer once show up in gym clothes to a lunch and nobody cared, but if it’s me, I feel like every little thing shows. So… ideal would be just not having that softness there, I guess. Looking sharper. More controlled.

You can’t sit there with a buyer at a steakhouse and make it weird, you know? They’re ordering appetizers, drinks, dessert, and you’re trying to keep the relationship smooth. But then later I’m not thinking about the account, I’m thinking about how to undo it. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look kind of soft or puffy. And if I think I do, it gets in my head that people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have control.\n\nWhich is probably unfair—like, I had this buyer once show up in gym clothes to a lunch and nobody cared, but if it’s me, I feel like every little thing shows. Because once I sit down and eat a full meal, it feels like I’ve blown the whole day, like I lost the one thing I was keeping under control.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of gaining weight affect other areas of your life beyond just what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I still do my route, I’m not falling apart at work or anything. Like client dinners are a pain now—unless I know the place or can sort of steer where we go, I’ll make an excuse. Same with dates, honestly. Which is weird because after the divorce everyone’s like, “Get back out there,” and my buddy keeps trying to set me up with this woman he knows, and I keep saying I’m slammed with work.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more… mostly when I can’t control what the food situation’s going to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… people keep saying I look too thin, so I know that’s what they think. My sister made this whole thing out of it last time I saw her, and even one of my accounts joked that I was “shrinking,” which was awkward.\n\nBut underweight, like medically? I don’t know. Yeah… I think I’d feel better, at least for a while, if I looked a certain way. Like sharper, I guess. Less… soft.

It’s weird because it’s not like I’m trying to look like some fitness model or anything. It’s more, if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date and I look in the mirror, I just go straight to my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits wrong, then I start thinking, “That’s what people are going to notice.” Like they’ll see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have my life together.

And then I’ll be distracted by that instead of whatever I’m supposed to be doing. I mean, I’ve had sales calls where the client is talking about inventory or delivery schedules, and I’m half listening because I’m wondering if my jacket is hiding my stomach enough. So yeah… if I could just look more controlled, I think I’d feel better. Maybe not completely better, but better.

Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of exactly what it feels like. Not logically, maybe, but in the moment it’s like the number means more than just weight.

Like if I have a client dinner and everyone’s ordering drinks and appetizers, or I’m in Miami for a weekend and it’s just… food and going out and not really having my normal routine, then if the scale is even one pound higher after, I get this panic. Like, “Okay, you let things get away from you.” And then my brain immediately goes to, “How do I fix this tomorrow? What do I cut back?”

It’s not just, “Oh, I gained a pound.” It feels like I’m losing control in general, like if I can’t manage that, then maybe I’m slipping in other ways too. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it hits me.

Which is weird because after the divorce everyone’s like, “Get back out there,” and my buddy keeps trying to set me up with this woman he knows, and I keep saying I’m slammed with work.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more… mostly when I can’t control what the food situation’s going to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… people keep saying I look too thin, so I know that’s what they think. My sister made this whole thing out of it last time I saw her, and even one of my accounts joked that I was “shrinking,” which was awkward.\n\nBut underweight, like medically? I don’t know. I still get up, I still jog, I’m still driving my territory and hitting my numbers. If I can do all that, it doesn’t really feel like some emergency, you know? Like, I’m functioning. So I guess I don’t totally see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal is kind of a weird word. Like, I know everyone’s making a big deal about it lately, and yeah, maybe I’m on the thinner side for my height.\n\nBut I don’t look at it like some medical emergency. I’m still jogging, I’m still out working my territory, seeing accounts, hitting my numbers. If I were really that far off, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to keep up with all that, you know?\n\nI had this one client last week—totally unrelated—but he was telling me how he’s trying to “get healthy” and cut carbs, and everyone acts like that’s discipline when it’s him. But with me, suddenly it’s a problem. So… I don’t know. Maybe not “normal” normal, but I don’t think it’s as bad as everyone keeps saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, obviously, but I do think I’m probably looking at different things than they are.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting, or especially a date, I’ll catch myself in the mirror and I’m not thinking, “Oh, you look fine.” I’m looking right at my stomach, or my face, like whether my face looks puffy or whether my shirt makes me look… I don’t know, soft. And if I get that in my head, it’s hard to shake. I start thinking people are going to read that as sloppy, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nWhich sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because most people are probably thinking about their own stuff. Like half my clients barely look up from their phones unless we’re talking pricing. But in the moment it feels like that’s what they’re going to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror or didn't have access to scales?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. I mean, the mirror and the scale make it worse, definitely, because then there’s like proof sitting there staring at me.\n\nBut even if I didn’t have them, I’d still be thinking about it. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve been out for a weekend in Miami or something, I can just feel this panic start up, like, “Okay, you overdid it, you’re losing control.” And if the scale is even one pound higher after that, it kind of confirms it, and then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nIt’s annoying because those dinners are part of the job sometimes. You can’t sit there with a buyer at a steakhouse and make it weird, you know? They’re ordering appetizers, drinks, dessert, and you’re trying to keep the relationship smooth. But then later I’m not thinking about the account, I’m thinking about how to undo it. So… yeah, I don’t think taking away the mirror would totally take away the worry.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you think would be ideal for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s like… a number where I’d say, “That’s perfect.” It’s more a look, I guess.\n\nLike before a meeting or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not looking at the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look kind of soft or puffy. And if I think I do, it gets in my head that people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have control.\n\nWhich is probably unfair—like, I had this buyer once show up in gym clothes to a lunch and nobody cared, but if it’s me, I feel like every little thing shows. So… ideal would be just not having that softness there, I guess. Looking sharper. More controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your weight if you could achieve a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d feel better, at least for a while, if I looked a certain way. Like sharper, I guess. Less… soft.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m trying to look like some fitness model or anything. It’s more, if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date and I look in the mirror, I just go straight to my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits wrong, then I start thinking, “That’s what people are going to notice.” Like they’ll see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have my life together.\n\nAnd then I’ll be distracted by that instead of whatever I’m supposed to be doing. I mean, I’ve had sales calls where the client is talking about inventory or delivery schedules, and I’m half listening because I’m wondering if my jacket is hiding my stomach enough. So yeah… if I could just look more controlled, I think I’d feel better. Maybe not completely better, but better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of exactly what it feels like. Not logically, maybe, but in the moment it’s like the number means more than just weight.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner and everyone’s ordering drinks and appetizers, or I’m in Miami for a weekend and it’s just… food and going out and not really having my normal routine, then if the scale is even one pound higher after, I get this panic. Like, “Okay, you let things get away from you.” And then my brain immediately goes to, “How do I fix this tomorrow? What do I cut back?”\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I gained a pound.” It feels like I’m losing control in general, like if I can’t manage that, then maybe I’m slipping in other ways too. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it hits me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess there is. It’s not like I have some exact… like, “I need to look like this,” but I’m trying to avoid looking soft, if that makes sense.

Like before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t even really look at the whole outfit. I just go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my stomach looks like it’s sticking out or my face looks puffy, then I start thinking people are going to notice that first.

And it turns into this whole thing in my head, like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not in control. Which is ridiculous, because half the time other people probably aren’t even paying attention. I mean, I’ve been in meetings where the buyer is wearing sneakers and looks like they just came from errands, and nobody cares. But with me, it feels like every little thing shows. So yeah… I think I’m trying to maintain that sharper look, just so I don’t feel like I’m gaining or losing control.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR’s been making comments, and my sister too—she keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin. I don’t really see it the same way. I mean, my suits fit sharper now, which in sales matters more than people admit. But yeah… my weight’s kept dropping since the divorce, and everyone’s acting like it’s some big alarm bell.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds dramatic, but yeah, kind of. If I get on the scale after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami and it’s even a pound up, I get this rush of panic, like I’ve messed everything up or I’m losing control. Then I’m already thinking about what I need to cut back the next day.\n\nWhich is stupid, because half the time those dinners are basically part of the job—everyone ordering appetizers, drinks, the whole show—and you can’t be the weird guy making a scene about it. But then afterward it’s all I can think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat due to this fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Most days I keep it pretty tight—coffee in the morning, then maybe a protein bar if I’m between sales calls and need something quick. Honestly, half the time I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy, which I am, like when I’m driving all over to meet accounts and you don’t want to show up smelling like takeout or whatever.\n\nBut then at night I’ll usually do something small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. Because once I sit down and eat a full meal, it feels like I’ve blown the whole day, like I lost the one thing I was keeping under control.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of gaining weight affect other areas of your life beyond just what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I still do my route, I’m not falling apart at work or anything. I’m still seeing accounts, making calls, doing the whole sales thing.\n\nBut it does kind of bleed into stuff. Like client dinners are a pain now—unless I know the place or can sort of steer where we go, I’ll make an excuse. Same with dates, honestly. Which is weird because after the divorce everyone’s like, “Get back out there,” and my buddy keeps trying to set me up with this woman he knows, and I keep saying I’m slammed with work.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more… mostly when I can’t control what the food situation’s going to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… people keep saying I look too thin, so I know that’s what they think. My sister made this whole thing out of it last time I saw her, and even one of my accounts joked that I was “shrinking,” which was awkward.\n\nBut underweight, like medically? I don’t know. I still get up, I still jog, I’m still driving my territory and hitting my numbers. If I can do all that, it doesn’t really feel like some emergency, you know? Like, I’m functioning. So I guess I don’t totally see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal is kind of a weird word. Like, I know everyone’s making a big deal about it lately, and yeah, maybe I’m on the thinner side for my height.\n\nBut I don’t look at it like some medical emergency. I’m still jogging, I’m still out working my territory, seeing accounts, hitting my numbers. If I were really that far off, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to keep up with all that, you know?\n\nI had this one client last week—totally unrelated—but he was telling me how he’s trying to “get healthy” and cut carbs, and everyone acts like that’s discipline when it’s him. But with me, suddenly it’s a problem. So… I don’t know. Maybe not “normal” normal, but I don’t think it’s as bad as everyone keeps saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, obviously, but I do think I’m probably looking at different things than they are.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting, or especially a date, I’ll catch myself in the mirror and I’m not thinking, “Oh, you look fine.” I’m looking right at my stomach, or my face, like whether my face looks puffy or whether my shirt makes me look… I don’t know, soft. And if I get that in my head, it’s hard to shake. I start thinking people are going to read that as sloppy, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nWhich sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because most people are probably thinking about their own stuff. Like half my clients barely look up from their phones unless we’re talking pricing. But in the moment it feels like that’s what they’re going to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror or didn't have access to scales?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. I mean, the mirror and the scale make it worse, definitely, because then there’s like proof sitting there staring at me.\n\nBut even if I didn’t have them, I’d still be thinking about it. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve been out for a weekend in Miami or something, I can just feel this panic start up, like, “Okay, you overdid it, you’re losing control.” And if the scale is even one pound higher after that, it kind of confirms it, and then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nIt’s annoying because those dinners are part of the job sometimes. You can’t sit there with a buyer at a steakhouse and make it weird, you know? They’re ordering appetizers, drinks, dessert, and you’re trying to keep the relationship smooth. But then later I’m not thinking about the account, I’m thinking about how to undo it. So… yeah, I don’t think taking away the mirror would totally take away the worry.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you think would be ideal for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s like… a number where I’d say, “That’s perfect.” It’s more a look, I guess.\n\nLike before a meeting or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not looking at the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look kind of soft or puffy. And if I think I do, it gets in my head that people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have control.\n\nWhich is probably unfair—like, I had this buyer once show up in gym clothes to a lunch and nobody cared, but if it’s me, I feel like every little thing shows. So… ideal would be just not having that softness there, I guess. Looking sharper. More controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your weight if you could achieve a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d feel better, at least for a while, if I looked a certain way. Like sharper, I guess. Less… soft.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m trying to look like some fitness model or anything. It’s more, if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date and I look in the mirror, I just go straight to my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits wrong, then I start thinking, “That’s what people are going to notice.” Like they’ll see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have my life together.\n\nAnd then I’ll be distracted by that instead of whatever I’m supposed to be doing. I mean, I’ve had sales calls where the client is talking about inventory or delivery schedules, and I’m half listening because I’m wondering if my jacket is hiding my stomach enough. So yeah… if I could just look more controlled, I think I’d feel better. Maybe not completely better, but better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of exactly what it feels like. Not logically, maybe, but in the moment it’s like the number means more than just weight.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner and everyone’s ordering drinks and appetizers, or I’m in Miami for a weekend and it’s just… food and going out and not really having my normal routine, then if the scale is even one pound higher after, I get this panic. Like, “Okay, you let things get away from you.” And then my brain immediately goes to, “How do I fix this tomorrow? What do I cut back?”\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I gained a pound.” It feels like I’m losing control in general, like if I can’t manage that, then maybe I’m slipping in other ways too. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific body shape or appearance that you're trying to maintain to avoid feeling like you're gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess there is. It’s not like I have some exact… like, “I need to look like this,” but I’m trying to avoid looking soft, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t even really look at the whole outfit. I just go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my stomach looks like it’s sticking out or my face looks puffy, then I start thinking people are going to notice that first.\n\nAnd it turns into this whole thing in my head, like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not in control. I mean, I’ve been in meetings where the buyer is wearing sneakers and looks like they just came from errands, and nobody cares. But with me, it feels like every little thing shows. So yeah… I think I’m trying to maintain that sharper look, just so I don’t feel like I’m gaining or losing control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not logically, maybe, but in the moment it’s like the number means more than just weight.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner and everyone’s ordering drinks and appetizers, or I’m in Miami for a weekend and it’s just… food and going out and not really having my normal routine, then if the scale is even one pound higher after, I get this panic. Like, “Okay, you let things get away from you.” And then my brain immediately goes to, “How do I fix this tomorrow? What do I cut back?”\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I gained a pound.” It feels like I’m losing control in general, like if I can’t manage that, then maybe I’m slipping in other ways too. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific body shape or appearance that you're trying to maintain to avoid feeling like you're gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess there is. It’s not like I have some exact… like, “I need to look like this,” but I’m trying to avoid looking soft, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t even really look at the whole outfit. I just go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my stomach looks like it’s sticking out or my face looks puffy, then I start thinking people are going to notice that first.\n\nAnd it turns into this whole thing in my head, like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not in control. Which is ridiculous, because half the time other people probably aren’t even paying attention. I mean, I’ve been in meetings where the buyer is wearing sneakers and looks like they just came from errands, and nobody cares. But with me, it feels like every little thing shows. So yeah… I think I’m trying to maintain that sharper look, just so I don’t feel like I’m gaining or losing control.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 13\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, it does feel that way. Like if the scale is even just a pound higher after, I don’t know, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami or something, I get this immediate panic.

And it’s annoying because one pound could be anything, right? Like salt, or eating late, or whatever people say. But in my head it’s not that reasonable. It turns into, “You messed up. You let things slide.” Then I’m already planning the next day, like what I’m going to cut back on to get it back under control.

It’s not just about the number, I guess. It feels like if that goes up, then maybe I’m not managing other things either. Which… I know sounds kind of intense, but that’s where my mind goes.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR’s been making comments, and my sister too—she keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin. I don’t really see it the same way. I mean, my suits fit sharper now, which in sales matters more than people admit. But yeah… my weight’s kept dropping since the divorce, and everyone’s acting like it’s some big alarm bell.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds dramatic, but yeah, kind of. If I get on the scale after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami and it’s even a pound up, I get this rush of panic, like I’ve messed everything up or I’m losing control. Then I’m already thinking about what I need to cut back the next day.\n\nWhich is stupid, because half the time those dinners are basically part of the job—everyone ordering appetizers, drinks, the whole show—and you can’t be the weird guy making a scene about it. But then afterward it’s all I can think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat due to this fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Most days I keep it pretty tight—coffee in the morning, then maybe a protein bar if I’m between sales calls and need something quick. Honestly, half the time I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy, which I am, like when I’m driving all over to meet accounts and you don’t want to show up smelling like takeout or whatever.\n\nBut then at night I’ll usually do something small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. Because once I sit down and eat a full meal, it feels like I’ve blown the whole day, like I lost the one thing I was keeping under control.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of gaining weight affect other areas of your life beyond just what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I still do my route, I’m not falling apart at work or anything. I’m still seeing accounts, making calls, doing the whole sales thing.\n\nBut it does kind of bleed into stuff. Like client dinners are a pain now—unless I know the place or can sort of steer where we go, I’ll make an excuse. Same with dates, honestly. Which is weird because after the divorce everyone’s like, “Get back out there,” and my buddy keeps trying to set me up with this woman he knows, and I keep saying I’m slammed with work.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more… mostly when I can’t control what the food situation’s going to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… people keep saying I look too thin, so I know that’s what they think. My sister made this whole thing out of it last time I saw her, and even one of my accounts joked that I was “shrinking,” which was awkward.\n\nBut underweight, like medically? I don’t know. I still get up, I still jog, I’m still driving my territory and hitting my numbers. If I can do all that, it doesn’t really feel like some emergency, you know? Like, I’m functioning. So I guess I don’t totally see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal is kind of a weird word. Like, I know everyone’s making a big deal about it lately, and yeah, maybe I’m on the thinner side for my height.\n\nBut I don’t look at it like some medical emergency. I’m still jogging, I’m still out working my territory, seeing accounts, hitting my numbers. If I were really that far off, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to keep up with all that, you know?\n\nI had this one client last week—totally unrelated—but he was telling me how he’s trying to “get healthy” and cut carbs, and everyone acts like that’s discipline when it’s him. But with me, suddenly it’s a problem. So… I don’t know. Maybe not “normal” normal, but I don’t think it’s as bad as everyone keeps saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, obviously, but I do think I’m probably looking at different things than they are.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting, or especially a date, I’ll catch myself in the mirror and I’m not thinking, “Oh, you look fine.” I’m looking right at my stomach, or my face, like whether my face looks puffy or whether my shirt makes me look… I don’t know, soft. And if I get that in my head, it’s hard to shake. I start thinking people are going to read that as sloppy, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nWhich sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because most people are probably thinking about their own stuff. Like half my clients barely look up from their phones unless we’re talking pricing. But in the moment it feels like that’s what they’re going to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror or didn't have access to scales?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. I mean, the mirror and the scale make it worse, definitely, because then there’s like proof sitting there staring at me.\n\nBut even if I didn’t have them, I’d still be thinking about it. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve been out for a weekend in Miami or something, I can just feel this panic start up, like, “Okay, you overdid it, you’re losing control.” And if the scale is even one pound higher after that, it kind of confirms it, and then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nIt’s annoying because those dinners are part of the job sometimes. You can’t sit there with a buyer at a steakhouse and make it weird, you know? They’re ordering appetizers, drinks, dessert, and you’re trying to keep the relationship smooth. But then later I’m not thinking about the account, I’m thinking about how to undo it. So… yeah, I don’t think taking away the mirror would totally take away the worry.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you think would be ideal for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s like… a number where I’d say, “That’s perfect.” It’s more a look, I guess.\n\nLike before a meeting or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not looking at the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look kind of soft or puffy. And if I think I do, it gets in my head that people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have control.\n\nWhich is probably unfair—like, I had this buyer once show up in gym clothes to a lunch and nobody cared, but if it’s me, I feel like every little thing shows. So… ideal would be just not having that softness there, I guess. Looking sharper. More controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your weight if you could achieve a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d feel better, at least for a while, if I looked a certain way. Like sharper, I guess. Less… soft.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m trying to look like some fitness model or anything. It’s more, if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date and I look in the mirror, I just go straight to my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits wrong, then I start thinking, “That’s what people are going to notice.” Like they’ll see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have my life together.\n\nAnd then I’ll be distracted by that instead of whatever I’m supposed to be doing. I mean, I’ve had sales calls where the client is talking about inventory or delivery schedules, and I’m half listening because I’m wondering if my jacket is hiding my stomach enough. So yeah… if I could just look more controlled, I think I’d feel better. Maybe not completely better, but better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of exactly what it feels like. Not logically, maybe, but in the moment it’s like the number means more than just weight.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner and everyone’s ordering drinks and appetizers, or I’m in Miami for a weekend and it’s just… food and going out and not really having my normal routine, then if the scale is even one pound higher after, I get this panic. Like, “Okay, you let things get away from you.” And then my brain immediately goes to, “How do I fix this tomorrow? What do I cut back?”\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I gained a pound.” It feels like I’m losing control in general, like if I can’t manage that, then maybe I’m slipping in other ways too. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific body shape or appearance that you're trying to maintain to avoid feeling like you're gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess there is. It’s not like I have some exact… like, “I need to look like this,” but I’m trying to avoid looking soft, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t even really look at the whole outfit. I just go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my stomach looks like it’s sticking out or my face looks puffy, then I start thinking people are going to notice that first.\n\nAnd it turns into this whole thing in my head, like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not in control. Which is ridiculous, because half the time other people probably aren’t even paying attention. I mean, I’ve been in meetings where the buyer is wearing sneakers and looks like they just came from errands, and nobody cares. But with me, it feels like every little thing shows. So yeah… I think I’m trying to maintain that sharper look, just so I don’t feel like I’m gaining or losing control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it does feel that way. Like if the scale is even just a pound higher after, I don’t know, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami or something, I get this immediate panic.\n\nAnd it’s annoying because one pound could be anything, right? If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits wrong, then I start thinking, “That’s what people are going to notice.” Like they’ll see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have my life together.\n\nAnd then I’ll be distracted by that instead of whatever I’m supposed to be doing. What do I cut back?”\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I gained a pound.” It feels like I’m losing control in general, like if I can’t manage that, then maybe I’m slipping in other ways too. I just go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my stomach looks like it’s sticking out or my face looks puffy, then I start thinking people are going to notice that first.\n\nAnd it turns into this whole thing in my head, like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not in control. Yeah… I don’t know if it’s one exact number or one exact shape, honestly. It’s more like there’s this line I feel like I can’t cross.

Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like, when I’m getting ready before a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I just go right to my stomach, or my face.

And if I think my stomach looks soft, or my face looks kind of puffy, then I start thinking that’s what everyone else is going to notice too. Like they’re going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have myself together.

Which is weird because in sales, you see all kinds of people and half of them aren’t thinking about that at all, they’re thinking about pricing or delivery dates or whatever. But with me, it feels like if my body looks a certain way, it says something bad about me. So yeah, I guess I’m afraid of how I’d look if I gained weight.

But then afterward it’s all I can think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat due to this fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Most days I keep it pretty tight—coffee in the morning, then maybe a protein bar if I’m between sales calls and need something quick. Honestly, half the time I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy, which I am, like when I’m driving all over to meet accounts and you don’t want to show up smelling like takeout or whatever.\n\nBut then at night I’ll usually do something small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. Because once I sit down and eat a full meal, it feels like I’ve blown the whole day, like I lost the one thing I was keeping under control.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of gaining weight affect other areas of your life beyond just what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I still do my route, I’m not falling apart at work or anything. I’m still seeing accounts, making calls, doing the whole sales thing.\n\nBut it does kind of bleed into stuff. Like client dinners are a pain now—unless I know the place or can sort of steer where we go, I’ll make an excuse. Same with dates, honestly. Which is weird because after the divorce everyone’s like, “Get back out there,” and my buddy keeps trying to set me up with this woman he knows, and I keep saying I’m slammed with work.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more… mostly when I can’t control what the food situation’s going to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… people keep saying I look too thin, so I know that’s what they think. My sister made this whole thing out of it last time I saw her, and even one of my accounts joked that I was “shrinking,” which was awkward.\n\nBut underweight, like medically? I don’t know. I still get up, I still jog, I’m still driving my territory and hitting my numbers. If I can do all that, it doesn’t really feel like some emergency, you know? Like, I’m functioning. So I guess I don’t totally see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal is kind of a weird word. Like, I know everyone’s making a big deal about it lately, and yeah, maybe I’m on the thinner side for my height.\n\nBut I don’t look at it like some medical emergency. I’m still jogging, I’m still out working my territory, seeing accounts, hitting my numbers. If I were really that far off, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to keep up with all that, you know?\n\nI had this one client last week—totally unrelated—but he was telling me how he’s trying to “get healthy” and cut carbs, and everyone acts like that’s discipline when it’s him. But with me, suddenly it’s a problem. So… I don’t know. Maybe not “normal” normal, but I don’t think it’s as bad as everyone keeps saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, obviously, but I do think I’m probably looking at different things than they are.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting, or especially a date, I’ll catch myself in the mirror and I’m not thinking, “Oh, you look fine.” I’m looking right at my stomach, or my face, like whether my face looks puffy or whether my shirt makes me look… I don’t know, soft. And if I get that in my head, it’s hard to shake. I start thinking people are going to read that as sloppy, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nWhich sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because most people are probably thinking about their own stuff. Like half my clients barely look up from their phones unless we’re talking pricing. But in the moment it feels like that’s what they’re going to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror or didn't have access to scales?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. I mean, the mirror and the scale make it worse, definitely, because then there’s like proof sitting there staring at me.\n\nBut even if I didn’t have them, I’d still be thinking about it. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve been out for a weekend in Miami or something, I can just feel this panic start up, like, “Okay, you overdid it, you’re losing control.” And if the scale is even one pound higher after that, it kind of confirms it, and then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nIt’s annoying because those dinners are part of the job sometimes. You can’t sit there with a buyer at a steakhouse and make it weird, you know? They’re ordering appetizers, drinks, dessert, and you’re trying to keep the relationship smooth. But then later I’m not thinking about the account, I’m thinking about how to undo it. So… yeah, I don’t think taking away the mirror would totally take away the worry.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you think would be ideal for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s like… a number where I’d say, “That’s perfect.” It’s more a look, I guess.\n\nLike before a meeting or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not looking at the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look kind of soft or puffy. And if I think I do, it gets in my head that people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have control.\n\nWhich is probably unfair—like, I had this buyer once show up in gym clothes to a lunch and nobody cared, but if it’s me, I feel like every little thing shows. So… ideal would be just not having that softness there, I guess. Looking sharper. More controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your weight if you could achieve a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d feel better, at least for a while, if I looked a certain way. Like sharper, I guess. Less… soft.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m trying to look like some fitness model or anything. It’s more, if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date and I look in the mirror, I just go straight to my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits wrong, then I start thinking, “That’s what people are going to notice.” Like they’ll see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have my life together.\n\nAnd then I’ll be distracted by that instead of whatever I’m supposed to be doing. I mean, I’ve had sales calls where the client is talking about inventory or delivery schedules, and I’m half listening because I’m wondering if my jacket is hiding my stomach enough. So yeah… if I could just look more controlled, I think I’d feel better. Maybe not completely better, but better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of exactly what it feels like. Not logically, maybe, but in the moment it’s like the number means more than just weight.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner and everyone’s ordering drinks and appetizers, or I’m in Miami for a weekend and it’s just… food and going out and not really having my normal routine, then if the scale is even one pound higher after, I get this panic. Like, “Okay, you let things get away from you.” And then my brain immediately goes to, “How do I fix this tomorrow? What do I cut back?”\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I gained a pound.” It feels like I’m losing control in general, like if I can’t manage that, then maybe I’m slipping in other ways too. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific body shape or appearance that you're trying to maintain to avoid feeling like you're gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess there is. It’s not like I have some exact… like, “I need to look like this,” but I’m trying to avoid looking soft, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t even really look at the whole outfit. I just go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my stomach looks like it’s sticking out or my face looks puffy, then I start thinking people are going to notice that first.\n\nAnd it turns into this whole thing in my head, like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not in control. Which is ridiculous, because half the time other people probably aren’t even paying attention. I mean, I’ve been in meetings where the buyer is wearing sneakers and looks like they just came from errands, and nobody cares. But with me, it feels like every little thing shows. So yeah… I think I’m trying to maintain that sharper look, just so I don’t feel like I’m gaining or losing control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it does feel that way. Like if the scale is even just a pound higher after, I don’t know, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami or something, I get this immediate panic.\n\nAnd it’s annoying because one pound could be anything, right? Like salt, or eating late, or whatever people say. But in my head it’s not that reasonable. It turns into, “You messed up. You let things slide.” Then I’m already planning the next day, like what I’m going to cut back on to get it back under control.\n\nIt’s not just about the number, I guess. It feels like if that goes up, then maybe I’m not managing other things either. Which… I know sounds kind of intense, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight tied to reaching a specific weight or body shape that you consider unacceptable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if it’s one exact number or one exact shape, honestly. It’s more like there’s this line I feel like I can’t cross.\n\nLike if I go to a client dinner, and it’s one of those places where everyone’s ordering for the table and you don’t want to be weird about it, or if I’m in Miami for a weekend and everything is eating late and drinks and whatever… if I come back and the scale is even a pound higher, I panic. It’s like, “Okay, you lost control.” And then I’m already thinking about the next day, like what I’m going to cut back on to fix it.\n\nSo I guess it’s tied to the scale, but not just the number. It’s the feeling that if it goes up at all, I’ve let myself slip.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you're afraid of how your body would look if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like, when I’m getting ready before a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I just go right to my stomach, or my face.\n\nAnd if I think my stomach looks soft, or my face looks kind of puffy, then I start thinking that’s what everyone else is going to notice too. Like they’re going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have myself together.\n\nWhich is weird because in sales, you see all kinds of people and half of them aren’t thinking about that at all, they’re thinking about pricing or delivery dates or whatever. But with me, it feels like if my body looks a certain way, it says something bad about me. So yeah, I guess I’m afraid of how I’d look if I gained weight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s like, “Okay, you lost control.” And then I’m already thinking about the next day, like what I’m going to cut back on to fix it.\n\nSo I guess it’s tied to the scale, but not just the number. It’s the feeling that if it goes up at all, I’ve let myself slip.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you're afraid of how your body would look if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it. It’s like, when I’m getting ready before a meeting or a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I just go right to my stomach, or my face.\n\nAnd if I think my stomach looks soft, or my face looks kind of puffy, then I start thinking that’s what everyone else is going to notice too. Like they’re going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have myself together.\n\nWhich is weird because in sales, you see all kinds of people and half of them aren’t thinking about that at all, they’re thinking about pricing or delivery dates or whatever. But with me, it feels like if my body looks a certain way, it says something bad about me. So yeah, I guess I’m afraid of how I’d look if I gained weight.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your body looks different than it actually does when you look in the mirror?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s hard to tell because in the moment it feels completely real.

Like if I’m getting ready before a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see, like, the whole person. I go straight to my stomach or my face. And if my stomach looks even a little soft, or my face looks puffy, then that’s all I can think about.

And then I start thinking other people are going to see that too, like I look sloppy or weak or like I don’t have myself together. Which is probably not what they’re focused on… like at work people are usually thinking about the deal or the order or whatever. But in my head, yeah, my body looks like this big obvious problem.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR’s been making comments, and my sister too—she keeps saying I’ve gotten too thin. I don’t really see it the same way. I mean, my suits fit sharper now, which in sales matters more than people admit. But yeah… my weight’s kept dropping since the divorce, and everyone’s acting like it’s some big alarm bell.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds dramatic, but yeah, kind of. If I get on the scale after, like, a client dinner or a weekend in Miami and it’s even a pound up, I get this rush of panic, like I’ve messed everything up or I’m losing control. Then I’m already thinking about what I need to cut back the next day.\n\nWhich is stupid, because half the time those dinners are basically part of the job—everyone ordering appetizers, drinks, the whole show—and you can’t be the weird guy making a scene about it. But then afterward it’s all I can think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat due to this fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Most days I keep it pretty tight—coffee in the morning, then maybe a protein bar if I’m between sales calls and need something quick. Honestly, half the time I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy, which I am, like when I’m driving all over to meet accounts and you don’t want to show up smelling like takeout or whatever.\n\nBut then at night I’ll usually do something small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. Because once I sit down and eat a full meal, it feels like I’ve blown the whole day, like I lost the one thing I was keeping under control.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of gaining weight affect other areas of your life beyond just what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I still do my route, I’m not falling apart at work or anything. I’m still seeing accounts, making calls, doing the whole sales thing.\n\nBut it does kind of bleed into stuff. Like client dinners are a pain now—unless I know the place or can sort of steer where we go, I’ll make an excuse. Same with dates, honestly. Which is weird because after the divorce everyone’s like, “Get back out there,” and my buddy keeps trying to set me up with this woman he knows, and I keep saying I’m slammed with work.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve been turning down more invitations and keeping to myself more… mostly when I can’t control what the food situation’s going to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… people keep saying I look too thin, so I know that’s what they think. My sister made this whole thing out of it last time I saw her, and even one of my accounts joked that I was “shrinking,” which was awkward.\n\nBut underweight, like medically? I don’t know. I still get up, I still jog, I’m still driving my territory and hitting my numbers. If I can do all that, it doesn’t really feel like some emergency, you know? Like, I’m functioning. So I guess I don’t totally see it the way everyone else seems to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal is kind of a weird word. Like, I know everyone’s making a big deal about it lately, and yeah, maybe I’m on the thinner side for my height.\n\nBut I don’t look at it like some medical emergency. I’m still jogging, I’m still out working my territory, seeing accounts, hitting my numbers. If I were really that far off, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to keep up with all that, you know?\n\nI had this one client last week—totally unrelated—but he was telling me how he’s trying to “get healthy” and cut carbs, and everyone acts like that’s discipline when it’s him. But with me, suddenly it’s a problem. So… I don’t know. Maybe not “normal” normal, but I don’t think it’s as bad as everyone keeps saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, obviously, but I do think I’m probably looking at different things than they are.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready for a meeting, or especially a date, I’ll catch myself in the mirror and I’m not thinking, “Oh, you look fine.” I’m looking right at my stomach, or my face, like whether my face looks puffy or whether my shirt makes me look… I don’t know, soft. And if I get that in my head, it’s hard to shake. I start thinking people are going to read that as sloppy, or like I don’t have control over myself.\n\nWhich sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because most people are probably thinking about their own stuff. Like half my clients barely look up from their phones unless we’re talking pricing. But in the moment it feels like that’s what they’re going to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror or didn't have access to scales?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would. I mean, the mirror and the scale make it worse, definitely, because then there’s like proof sitting there staring at me.\n\nBut even if I didn’t have them, I’d still be thinking about it. Like after a client dinner, or if I’ve been out for a weekend in Miami or something, I can just feel this panic start up, like, “Okay, you overdid it, you’re losing control.” And if the scale is even one pound higher after that, it kind of confirms it, and then I start planning how I’m going to cut back the next day.\n\nIt’s annoying because those dinners are part of the job sometimes. You can’t sit there with a buyer at a steakhouse and make it weird, you know? They’re ordering appetizers, drinks, dessert, and you’re trying to keep the relationship smooth. But then later I’m not thinking about the account, I’m thinking about how to undo it. So… yeah, I don’t think taking away the mirror would totally take away the worry.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you think would be ideal for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s like… a number where I’d say, “That’s perfect.” It’s more a look, I guess.\n\nLike before a meeting or if I’m going on a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not looking at the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look kind of soft or puffy. And if I think I do, it gets in my head that people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have control.\n\nWhich is probably unfair—like, I had this buyer once show up in gym clothes to a lunch and nobody cared, but if it’s me, I feel like every little thing shows. So… ideal would be just not having that softness there, I guess. Looking sharper. More controlled.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your weight if you could achieve a certain body shape or appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d feel better, at least for a while, if I looked a certain way. Like sharper, I guess. Less… soft.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I’m trying to look like some fitness model or anything. It’s more, if I’m getting ready for a meeting or a date and I look in the mirror, I just go straight to my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits wrong, then I start thinking, “That’s what people are going to notice.” Like they’ll see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I don’t have my life together.\n\nAnd then I’ll be distracted by that instead of whatever I’m supposed to be doing. I mean, I’ve had sales calls where the client is talking about inventory or delivery schedules, and I’m half listening because I’m wondering if my jacket is hiding my stomach enough. So yeah… if I could just look more controlled, I think I’d feel better. Maybe not completely better, but better.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of exactly what it feels like. Not logically, maybe, but in the moment it’s like the number means more than just weight.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner and everyone’s ordering drinks and appetizers, or I’m in Miami for a weekend and it’s just… food and going out and not really having my normal routine, then if the scale is even one pound higher after, I get this panic. Like, “Okay, you let things get away from you.” And then my brain immediately goes to, “How do I fix this tomorrow? What do I cut back?”\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, I gained a pound.” It feels like I’m losing control in general, like if I can’t manage that, then maybe I’m slipping in other ways too. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific body shape or appearance that you're trying to maintain to avoid feeling like you're gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess there is. It’s not like I have some exact… like, “I need to look like this,” but I’m trying to avoid looking soft, if that makes sense.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t even really look at the whole outfit. I just go straight to my stomach, or my face. If my stomach looks like it’s sticking out or my face looks puffy, then I start thinking people are going to notice that first.\n\nAnd it turns into this whole thing in my head, like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not in control. Which is ridiculous, because half the time other people probably aren’t even paying attention. I mean, I’ve been in meetings where the buyer is wearing sneakers and looks like they just came from errands, and nobody cares. But with me, it feels like every little thing shows. So yeah… I think I’m trying to maintain that sharper look, just so I don’t feel like I’m gaining or losing control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like gaining weight would mean you're losing control over other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it does feel that way. Like if the scale is even just a pound higher after, I don’t know, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami or something, I get this immediate panic.\n\nAnd it’s annoying because one pound could be anything, right? Like salt, or eating late, or whatever people say. But in my head it’s not that reasonable. It turns into, “You messed up. You let things slide.” Then I’m already planning the next day, like what I’m going to cut back on to get it back under control.\n\nIt’s not just about the number, I guess. It feels like if that goes up, then maybe I’m not managing other things either. Which… I know sounds kind of intense, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight tied to reaching a specific weight or body shape that you consider unacceptable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if it’s one exact number or one exact shape, honestly. It’s like, “Okay, you lost control.” And then I’m already thinking about the next day, like what I’m going to cut back on to fix it.\n\nSo I guess it’s tied to the scale, but not just the number. It’s the feeling that if it goes up at all, I’ve let myself slip.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you're afraid of how your body would look if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a big part of it.
